Multilevel Picard scheme for solving high-dimensional drift control problems with state constraints
Yuan Zhong

Abstract
Motivated by applications to the dynamic control of queueing networks, we develop a simulation-based scheme, the so-called multilevel Picard (MLP) approximation, for solving high-dimensional drift control problems whose states are constrained to stay within the nonnegative orthant, over a finite time horizon. We prove that under suitable conditions, the MLP approximation overcomes the curse of dimensionality in the following sense: To approximate the value function and its gradient evaluated at a given time and state to within a prescribed accuracy ε \varepsilon , the computational complexity grows at most polynomially in the problem dimension d d and 1 / ε 1/\varepsilon . To illustrate the effectiveness of the scheme, we carry out numerical experiments for a class of test problems that are related to the dynamic scheduling problem of parallel server systems in heavy traffic, and demonstrate that the scheme is computationally feasible up to dimension at least 20 20 .



1
Introduction

Drift controls are stochastic control problems where the system state can be adjusted at finite rates.
They arise in a wide range of application domains, including manufacturing
[
95
,
82
]
, communications
[
8
]
,
service systems
[
12
,
11
,
74
]
, portfolio management
[
28
]
, and economics
[
14
]
.
In many of these problems, state constraints are a natural and critical feature;
for example, in service and manufacturing systems, job or customer counts are nonnegative,
and in portfolio management, the investor’s portfolio is commonly required to stay within a solvency region.

In this paper, we study problems in which states are constrained to stay within the nonnegative orthant
by a “reflecting” mechanism at the boundary
(henceforth, we refer to these as
drift controls with reflections
); See Section
3
for details.
Our primary motivation comes from the problem of dynamically controlling queueing networks in heavy traffic;
see e.g.,
[
9
,
108
]
and the references therein for recent overviews of
the extensive history of heavy-traffic analysis of queueing networks,
and its connections to and synergy with drift controls with reflections.

Except in a few special cases, drift control problems (with reflections) are difficult to solve analytically,
necessitating numerical solutions.
A standard approach is to consider the associated Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman (HJB) equations,
which are partial differential equations (PDEs) that characterize the value function of the control problem.
Classical grid-based solvers for these PDEs, such as finite-element and finite-difference methods
[
104
,
13
]
,
suffer from the curse of dimensionality and quickly become computationally intractable as the problem dimension grows; cf.
[
19
,
71
]
.
However, high-dimensional instances of these control problems arise in important practical applications
[
51
,
12
,
9
]
,
rendering the use of grid-based methods infeasible.
Another popular approach is the Markov chain approximation method (see, e.g.,
[
75
]
),
which solves carefully designed controlled Markov chains that approximate the original control problem,
but it also suffers from the curse of dimensionality, since the method requires fine discretization of the state space, similar to grid-based methods.

In this paper, we develop, analyze and implement a multilevel Picard (MLP) method for solving high-dimensional drift control problems with reflections.
MLP is a non-parametric, simulation-based framework that has been developed recently for solving broad classes of high-dimensional semilinear PDEs

[
69
,
70
,
85
,
87
,
47
,
15
,
17
,
39
,
64
,
68
,
62
,
66
,
67
,
86
]

– PDEs with nonlinearities only in lower-order terms –
and has been shown to provably overcome the curse of dimensionality under suitable conditions,
in the sense that the computational complexity scales polynomially in the problem dimension and
1/\varepsilon
,
where
\varepsilon
is the prescribed accuracy.
In contrast to existing literature, the presence of state constraints introduces considerable challenges to the analysis and implementation of the MLP method.
In particular, our approach relies heavily on so-called derivative processes of reflected diffusions
[
78
,
80
]
,
which capture the sensitivity of the state process to its defining parameters, such as changes in the initial state.
These processes, unlike those of unconstrained diffusions prevalent in prior works,
feature discontinuous sample paths and a discontinuous dependence on the initial state at the boundary.
To address these issues, we introduce new analytical and simulation tools and make the following contributions:

(i)

Establish a Bismut–Elworthy–Li (BEL) formula for reflected diffusions (Theorem
8.1
);

(ii)

Prove continuous dependence on the initial condition for the derivative processes, in the interior of the state space (Proposition
4.25
);

(iii)

Characterize the value function and its gradient for drift control problems with reflections as the unique fixed point of a contractive functional operator (Theorem
5.5
);

(iv)

Develop an MLP estimator for the value function and gradient at a given time–state pair, with computational complexity growing polynomially in the problem dimension and in
1/\varepsilon
, for prescribed error
\varepsilon
(Section
6
); and

(v)

Carry out numerical experiments that demonstrate computational feasibility and competitive empirical performance of the MLP scheme in dimension up to
20
(Section
7
).

Let us also briefly remark that besides MLP, other competitive approaches,
including several recently developed numerical methods that are deep-learning based, such as the deep backward stochastic differential equation (BSDE)
[
51
]
and deep splitting methods
[
16
]
,
have shown strong empirical performances in solving high-dimensional PDEs
[
51
,
16
,
61
,
41
,
9
,
4
,
6
]
.
Different from the MLP scheme developed in this paper, these methods are parametric and often require extensive engineering and hyperparameter tuning in order to work well.

1.1
Notation

We summarize the notation used throughout the paper.
Let
\mathbb{Z}
denote the set of integers,
\mathbb{N}
the set of positive integers,

\mathbb{R}
the set of real numbers,
\mathbb{R}_{+}
the set of nonnegative real numbers,
and
\mathbb{R}_{++}
the set of positive real numbers.
For two real numbers
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
, we use
\alpha_{1}\vee\alpha_{2}
to denote the maximum of
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
.
For
d\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbb{R}^{d}
denotes the
d
-dimensional Euclidean space,

\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}:=\{x=(x_{i})_{i=1}^{d}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:x_{i}\geq 0~~\forall i\}
denotes the nonnegative orthant,
and
\mathbb{R}_{++}^{d}:=\{x=(x_{i})_{i=1}^{d}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:x_{i}>0~~\forall i\}
denotes the positive orthant.
For a vector
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we use
x^{\top}
to denote its transpose,
and
\|x\|_{1}
,
\|x\|_{2}
and
\|x\|_{\infty}
to denote its
\ell_{1}
norm, Euclidean norm and
\ell_{\infty}
norm, respectively.
For vectors
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we use both
x^{\top}y
and
\langle x,y\rangle
to denote the inner product of
x
and
y
.
The
d
-dimensional vector of all ones is denoted by
\bm{1}_{d}
, and when the context is clear, we drop the subscript and use the notation
\bm{1}
.
The
j
th standard unit vector in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is denoted by
e_{j}
, with all components being zero but the
j
th component being one,
j=1,\cdots,d
,
For a matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times K}
,
d,K\in\mathbb{N}
, we use
\text{Tr}(A)
to denote the trace of
A
,

A^{\top}
to denote its transpose, and, with a slight abuse of notation,

\|A\|_{2}
and
\|A\|_{\infty}
to denote the operator norms induced by the Euclidean norm
\|\cdot\|_{2}

and by the
\ell_{\infty}
-norm
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
on
\mathbb{R}^{K}
, respectively
(see e.g., Definition 5.6.1 in Chapter 5 of
[
60
]
).
The indicator function is denoted by
\mathbbm{1}\{\cdot\}
.
A function
f:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is called a
d
-dimensional path, with the interpretation that
f(t)
is the state of path
f
at time
t
.
The space of càdlàg (or RCLL, which stands for right-continuous with left-limits) paths is denoted by
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
and the space of continuous paths is denoted by
\mathbb{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
We use
\mathbb{C}_{+}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d})
to denote the set of paths
f\in\mathbb{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
f(0)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
.
We also use
\mathbb{D}_{\text{lim}}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d})
to denote the set of paths
f
that have finite left limits at all
t>0

and finite right limits at all
t\geq 0
, and
\mathbb{D}_{l,r}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d})\subset\mathbb{D}_{\text{lim}}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d})

to denote the set of paths
f\in\mathbb{D}_{\text{lim}}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d})
that are either left-continuous or right-continuous at all
t>0
.
For
f\in\mathbb{D}_{\text{lim}}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d})
and
T>0
, we define the sup norm of
f
on
[0,T]
by

\|f\|_{T}:=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|f(t)\|_{\infty}.

(1)

For a domain
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
C^{k}(U)
denotes the space of continuous functions
u:U\to\mathbb{R}
that are
\ell
-times continuously differentiable.
We also use the notation
C^{1,2}([0,T]\times U)
to denote the space of continuous functions
u:[0,T]\times U\to\mathbb{R}
that are continuously differentiable in
t
and twice continuously differentiable in
x\in U

(
T=\infty
is allowed here),
and
C^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}\times{\cal A})
to denote the space of continuous functions
u:\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}\times{\cal A}\to\mathbb{R}
that are continuously differentiable in
x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
and
a\in{\cal A}
,
for a domain
{\cal A}\subset\mathbb{R}^{K}
for some
K\in\mathbb{N}
.
For a real-valued function
V:\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, if exist, the state gradient and Hessian of
V
with respect to
x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
are
D_{x}V
and
D_{xx}V
, respectively.
For a
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued function
\tilde{b}:\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
, we use
D\tilde{b}(x)
to denote the Jacobian matrix of
\tilde{b}
at
x
,
i.e.,

D\tilde{b}(x)=\left(\frac{\partial\tilde{b}_{i}(x)}{\partial x_{j}}\right)_{i=1,\cdots,d_{2},j=1,\cdots,d_{1}}.

1.2
Solution Approach Overview

We present an overview of our solution approach, highlighting key challenges and technical contributions,
without going into all technical details.
We start with an informal description of the control problem. Let
T>0
be fixed.
The state
Z(\cdot)
evolves according to the following
d
-dimensional diffusion process with reflections:

Z(t)=Z(0)+\int_{0}^{t}b\bigl(Z(s),a(s)\bigr)\,ds+\sigma B(t)+RY(t),\quad t\in[0,T],

(2)

with
Z(0)
being the initial state,
\sigma B(\cdot)
a
d
-dimensional Brownian motion with zero drift and covariance matrix
\sigma\sigma^{\top}
,

R
the reflection matrix, and
Y(\cdot)
the Skorokhod regulator that keeps
Z(\cdot)
in the nonnegative orthant;
see Sections
3
and
4.1
for details.
Here,
a(\cdot)
is the control process taking values in a compact action set
{\cal A}
,
which can adjust the drift coefficient
b(\cdot,a(\cdot))
, and in turn, the state process
Z(\cdot)
.
For
(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
, we wish to compute the value function
V(t,x)
, defined as the infimum of

J(t,x,a)
over all non-anticipating control processes
a(\cdot)
,
where
J(t,x,a)
is the total expected discounted cost over the time horizon
[t,T]
starting from
Z(t)=x
, under control
a(\cdot)
:

J(x,a):=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}e^{-\beta(s-t)}\,c\bigl(Z(s),a(s)\bigr)\,ds+\int_{t}^{T}e^{-\beta(s-t)}\,\kappa^{\top}dY(s)+e^{-\beta(T-t)}\,\xi(Z(T))\Big|Z(t)=x\right].

(3)

Here,
\beta
is the discount rate, and the total cost consists of three components: the running cost
c
,
which depends on both the state and control; the penalty costs
\kappa_{j}
, incurred whenever the
j
th component of the state
hits zero; and the terminal cost
\xi
.

A priori
, it is unclear whether the value function
V(t,x)
is differentiable in
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}
.
In the sequel we will show that under suitable conditions, this is the case, but assuming sufficient smoothness,
central to the development of a MLP scheme is a fixed-point formulation of the (state) gradient of the value function
D_{x}V

(see Eqs. (
13
) and (
14
)), which we now formally derive.

First, the following formal parabolic HJB equation for the value function
V
is standard:

V_{t}(t,x)+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{Tr}\left(\sigma\sigma^{\top}D_{xx}V(t,x)\right)+{\cal H}\left(x,D_{x}V(t,x)\right)-\beta V(t,x)=0,\quad(t,x)\in[0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+},

(4)

with terminal condition

V(T,x)=\xi(x),\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+},

(5)

and oblique derivative boundary conditions

\langle R_{j},D_{x}V(t,x)\rangle=-\kappa_{j},\quad\text{if }x_{j}=0,t<T.

(6)

The
Hamiltonian

{\cal H}
is given by

{\cal H}(x,p):=\inf_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\left\{(b(x,a)^{\top}p+c(x,a)\right\},\quad(x,p)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(7)

It is useful to note that given a function
\tilde{b}:\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the following equation is formally equivalent to Eq. (
4
):

V_{t}(t,x)+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{Tr}\left(\sigma\sigma^{\top}D_{xx}V(t,x)\right)+\tilde{b}(x)^{\top}D_{x}V(t,x)+\tilde{\cal H}\left(x,D_{x}V(t,x)\right)-\beta V(t,x)=0,

(8)

where the Hamiltonian
\tilde{\cal H}
is given by

\tilde{\cal H}(x,p):=\inf_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\left\{(b(x,a)-\tilde{b}(x))^{\top}p+c(x,a)\right\}.

(9)

Second, assuming that
V
is sufficiently smooth, given
t\in[0,T)
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}
, we apply Itô’s lemma for reflected diffusions (see, e.g.,
[
53
]
)
to the function
(s,z)\mapsto e^{-\beta(s-t)}V(s,z)

over the reference process
\tilde{Z}^{t,x}
to obtain the following Feynman-Kac representation for
V(t,x)
, making use of
the HJB equation (
8
), (
5
), and (
6
):

\displaystyle V(t,x)=

\displaystyle~\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}e^{-\beta(s-t)}\tilde{\cal H}\left(\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(s),D_{x}V(s,\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(s))\right)ds\right.

\displaystyle~~+\left.\int_{t}^{T}e^{-\beta(s-t)}\kappa^{\top}d\tilde{Y}^{t,x}(s)+e^{-\beta(T-t)}\xi(\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(T))\right],

(10)

where the process
\tilde{Z}^{t,x}
is a reflected diffusion with drift term
\tilde{b}
and diffusion coefficient
\sigma
starting with
\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(t)=x
,
i.e., it satisfies the following stochastic differential equation (SDE) with reflections:

\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(s)=x+\int_{t}^{s}\tilde{b}(\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(r))dr+\sigma(B(s)-B(t))+R\tilde{Y}^{t,x}(s),\quad s\in[t,T],

(11)

and
\tilde{Y}^{t,x}
is the Skorokhod regulator of the process
\tilde{Z}^{t,x}
.

Third, to derive a fixed-point characterization of the value function gradient
D_{x}V
from the Feynman-Kac representation (
10
),
which relates
V
and
D_{x}V
, we formally differentiate both sides of Eq. (
10
) with respect to
x
to obtain
D_{x}V
on the left-hand side.
On the right-hand side, instead of applying the chain rule, we apply a Bismut-Elworthy-Li type formula for reflected diffusions (see Section
8
for details),
and introduce a Malliavin weight of the form

\frac{1}{s-t}\int_{t}^{s}\left[\sigma^{-1}D\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(s)\right]^{\top}dB(s)

(12)

to the right-hand side of Eq. (
10
).
Here,
D\tilde{Z}^{t,x}
is the derivative process of
\tilde{Z}^{t,x}
with respect to
x
,
which takes values in
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and captures sensitivity of
\tilde{Z}^{t,x}
with respect to changes in
x
;
see Section
4
for precise definitions and detailed properties.

The end result is the following formal, functional fixed-point equation for
D_{x}V
:

D_{x}V(t,x)=F(D_{x}V)(t,x),\quad(t,x)\in[0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+},

(13)

where the functional map
F
is formally given by

\displaystyle F(v)(t,x):=~\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\int_{t}^{T}e^{-\beta(s-t)}\tilde{\cal H}\left(\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(s),v(s,\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(s))\right)\left(\frac{1}{s-t}\int_{t}^{s}\left[\sigma^{-1}D\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(r)\right]^{\top}dB(r)\right)ds

\displaystyle~~~~+\int_{t}^{T}e^{-\beta(s-t)}\kappa^{\top}d\left[D\tilde{Y}^{t,x}(s)\right]+e^{-\beta(T-t)}\xi(\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(T))\left(\frac{1}{T-t}\int_{t}^{T}\left[\sigma^{-1}D\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(r)\right]^{\top}dB(r)\right)\Bigg].

(14)

Here,
D\tilde{Y}^{t,x}
is the derivative process of
\tilde{Y}^{t,x}
with respect to
x
; also see Section
4
for details.
Note the insertions of Malliavin weights of the form (
12
) when comparing the definition of
F
in Eq. (
14
)
with the Feynman-Kac representation in Eq. (
10
).

So far, we have argued that if exists,
D_{x}V
should formally satisfy the functional fixed-point equation Eq. (
13
).
Using properties of the derivative processes
D\tilde{Z}^{t,x}
,
we are able to prove that
F
is a well-defined contraction mapping from an appropriate Banach space of functions into itself;
see Proposition
5.3
. It then follows from Banach’s fixed-point theorem that the mapping
F
has a unique fixed point,
and that, assuming
D_{x}V
exists and coincides with that fixed point, it can be approximated by
Picard iterations of the form
v^{(n)}=F(v^{(n-1)})
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Because it is computationally challenging to directly implement the iterative scheme (see, e.g., discussion in Section 2.1 of
[
39
]
),
we rewrite the iteration
v^{(n)}=F(v^{(n-1)})
as a telescopic sum,
so that for a given
(t,x)\in[0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}
, we have

v^{(n)}(t,x)=F(v^{(0)})(t,x)+\sum_{\ell=1}^{n-1}\left[F(v^{(\ell)})-F(v^{(\ell-1)})\right](t,x),

(15)

and approximate the differences
\left[F(v^{(\ell)})-F(v^{(\ell-1)})\right](t,x)

by a combination of Monte Carlo simulations and further Picard iterations;
the reason for further iterations is that
v^{(\ell)}
and
v^{(\ell-1)}
are not
a priori
known, so they themselves need to be approximated
at random time-state pairs, and so on and so forth.
In this sense, MLP is a recursive scheme that requires full history of all earlier levels
when estimating each of the differences
\left[F(v^{(\ell)})-F(v^{(\ell-1)})\right](t,x)
.
A key insight from the MLP literature is that as the level
\ell
increases, because of the contractive property of
F
,
the difference
\left[F(v^{(\ell)})-F(v^{(\ell-1)})\right]
becomes exponentially smaller under appropriate norms,
so a smaller number of samples suffice to approximate the difference well.
By carefully choosing geometrically decreasing numbers of samples to use at larger levels, it can be established that
under appropriate conditions, the total number of samples required to approximate the fixed point of
F
at a given time-state pair
(t,x)

to within
\varepsilon
prescribed error is polynomial in
d
and
1/\varepsilon
; see Section
6
for details.

To summarize, we have (a) outlined a formal argument deriving the functional fixed-point equation Eq. (
13
) for
D_{x}V
,
and (b) described the high-level intuitions behind the MLP scheme for approximating the unique fixed point at a given time-state pair
(t,x)
,
but an important missing link is to rigorously establish that
D_{x}V
is well-defined and is none other than the unique fixed point of
F
.
A standard approach is to use the viscosity solution paradigm
[
43
,
27
]
, but techniques in existing literature cannot be directly applied,
because they rely on the fact that unconstrained diffusions have continuous sample paths, and that their derivative processes depend continuously on initial conditions,
both properties of which do not hold for reflected diffusions.
To tackle these challenges, we develop two technical tools that are, to the best of our knowledge, new:
First, a Bismut-Elworthy-Li formula for reflected diffusions,
and second, continuous dependence of the derivative process on the initial condition,
in the interior of the state space.
Using these tools, we are able to prove that the antiderivative of the unique fixed point of
F

is a viscosity solution of the HJB equation Eq. (
4
) – (
6
).
By standard arguments, it can also be established that the value function
V
is a viscosity solution,
and that viscosity solutions are unique, so
V
must be differentiable and coincides with the antiderivative of the unique fixed point of
F
.
Through these results, we fully justify the use of the MLP scheme to approximate
D_{x}V
and
V
.

1.3
Organization

The rest of the paper is organized as follows.
Section
2
reviews related literature on drift controls, MLP methods for high-dimensional PDEs, and reflected diffusions and their derivative processes.
Section
3
provides a complete problem description, together with several standing assumptions used throughout the paper.
Section
4
collects technical preliminaries on the Skorokhod problem, reflected diffusions and their derivative processes.
Here we recall fundamental concepts and properties established in prior literature, and develop some new results that are used later for our analysis.
We also briefly recall the definition of viscosity solutions for parabolic PDEs with oblique derivative boundary conditions at the end of the section.
We state the first main result of the paper, which provides a fixed-point characterization of the value function gradient, in Section
5
.
Then, in Section
6
, we develop the multilevel Picard method, and state the sample complexity bounds, our second main result.
We conduct a set of numerical experiments to demonstrate the effectiveness of the MLP method in Section
7
.
The rest of the paper is devoted to proofs of various results:
Section
8
establishes a Bismut–Elworthy–Li formula for reflected diffusions, and
Section
9
establishes the fixed point of
F
as a viscosity solution of the HJB equation.
We conclude the paper in Section
10
with a brief summary and some future directions.
Further proofs, implementation details of the MLP method, and numerical results are contained in the Appendix.

2
Literature Review

The following streams of literature are most relevant to our work: (i) drift control problems; (ii) multilevel Picard approximations for solving PDEs;
and (iii) reflected diffusions and derivative processes. We also briefly review related literature on dynamic control of queueing networks in heavy traffic
in our discussion of drift control problems.

Drift control problems.

Several works consider one-dimensional drift control problems.
Due to their analytical tractability, it is often possible to derive closed-form solutions that can provide useful insights.
Motivated by applications to power control in wireless communication,
[
8
]
derives optimal policies in closed form
for a one-dimensional drift control problem on a bounded interval with general control costs but zero cost on states.
Extensions to the model in
[
8
]
are analyzed in
[
45
,
46
]
.

[
82
,
29
]
consider models in which fixed costs are incurred to change the drift, and derive optimality of control-band policies,
with applications to capacity management in a build-to-order environment.
Drift control problems also see applications to make-to-order manufacturing with cancellations
[
95
]
,
staffing of volunteer gleaners
[
7
]
, and foreign reserve holdings
[
14
]
.
Our work focuses on multidimensional settings where closed-form solutions are usually unavailable.

There is also extensive literature on multi-dimensional drift control problems, with and without state constraints. Motivated by applications to service systems,

[
11
,
52
]
analyze a queueing system with multiple customer classes and a single pool of servers that are capable of serving all customer types.
Both papers study a formal diffusion approximation to the dynamic control problem of the queueing system, in a so-called Halfin-Whitt regime, and derive structural properties
of the associated HJB equation, such as solution regularity. The diffusion approximation is a multi-dimensional drift control problem with no state constraints,
i.e., the state space is the entire Euclidean space. In addition,

[
11
]
proves asymptotic optimality of scheduling policies that are derived based on solutions to the HJB equation in the diffusion limit,
and
[
52
]
carried out numerical experiments in dimension
2
.

[
12
]
considers a significant generalization of the drift control problem in
[
11
,
52
]
,
which arises as the formal diffusion approximation of a queueing system
with multiple customer classes and multiple server pools, and derive and study properties of the corresponding HJB equation.

[
6
]
considers a model that is similar to that considered in
[
11
,
52
]
, and
develops a simulation-based computational method to solve the associated HJB equation, for problems up to dimension
500
.

Drift control problems with state constraints arise across domains, notably in queueing theory and mathematical finance.
As noted in the introduction, portfolio management often imposes a solvency constraint,
yielding control problems with absorbing boundaries in which the state process is stopped upon hitting the boundary.

[
28
]
develops a penalty method for portfolio problems with proportional transaction costs:
The associated singular control problem, in which system states may be instantaneously displaced, is approximated
by drift control problems, the latter of which are then solved by finite differences;
numerical results are reported for problems with dimension up to
2
.
In queueing theory, a primary motivation for our work, job or customer counts are nonnegative,
so servers must idle when the corresponding buffers are empty.
In heavy-traffic limits, diffusion approximations of such systems under fixed scheduling policies often lead to reflected diffusions
[
94
,
25
,
107
]
,
where a regulator process keeps the state within the feasible region;
server idling corresponds, roughly speaking, to the boundary local time.
When dynamic controls are allowed, the corresponding diffusion approximations are often drift controls with reflections (see, e.g.,
[
24
,
22
]
),
or singular controls with reflections (see, e.g.,
[
10
,
92
,
57
,
55
,
56
]
).
In the ergodic setting,
[
22
]
analyzes the viscosity solution properties of the value function,
while
[
24
]
justifies the diffusion approximation by proving convergence of the value functions in the heavy-traffic limit.
In another stream of literature (see, e.g.,
[
57
,
55
,
56
]
),
Brownian control problems (BCPs) are developed to approximate queueing network control problems in heavy traffic,
which can often be reduced to a lower-dimensional equivalent workload formulation (EWF).
These EWFs are typically singular control problems constrained to remain in a convex polyhedral domain with oblique reflection at the boundary.
Similar to drift controls that arise as approximations to singular controls in portfolio management problems,
drift controls with reflections can serve as tractable proxies for EWFs.

Much of prior works on drift controls with reflections focuses on their theoretical properties.
More recently,
[
9
]
develop a simulation-based computational method
to solve high-dimensional drift control problems with reflections up to problem dimension at least
30
, building on the deep BSDE method
pioneered in
[
51
]
. Their problem setting is similar to ours, but differ substantially in the solution approaches.
First, the method developed in
[
9
]
is deep-learning based, hence parametric,
but ours is non-parametric. Second, we focus on sample complexity guarantees for value function and gradient estimates with prescribed accuracy,
whereas
[
9
]
follows a “benchmarking” approach that focuses on matching optimal performances or beating existing benchmarks.
Finally, a key methodological difference is that
[
9
]
relies on a BSDE representation involving both the value function and its gradient,
while we rely on a functional fixed-point equation that builds on a Feynman-Kac representation and a Bismut-Elworthy-Li type formula.

As mentioned, drift control problems are closely related to singular control problems. Indeed, many singular control problems can be approximated
by drift controls
[
28
,
83
,
109
]
, opening the door for numerical solutions to singular control problems
via those of the approximating drift controls; see
[
5
]
for a computational method that can successfully solve high-dimensional singular control problems
with state constraints, which is based on solving the approximating drift controls.
In this vein, our method may potentially be used to produce useful benchmarks and/or approximate solutions for related singular control problems with reflections; see, e.g.,
[
10
,
92
]
.

Multilevel Picard approximation.

Our work builds on the MLP framework to solve drift control problems with reflections,
so it also contributes to and is related to the rapidly expanding literature on using multilevel Picard approximations to
solve high-dimensional PDEs; see
[
47
,
15
,
17
,
39
,
64
,
68
,
62
,
66
,
67
,
86
]
.
The MLP framework delicately combines several powerful ideas in and of themselves,
including nonlinear Feynman-Kac representations
[
89
,
91
,
90
]
, Bismut-Elworthy-Li type identities in the case of gradient-dependent nonlinearities
[
20
,
42
]
,
Picard iterations for BSDEs (see, e.g., Chapter 7 of
[
110
]
), and multilevel Monte Carlo simulations
[
48
,
49
]
.
MLP methods admit rigorous convergence and complexity analysis and, under standard Lipschitz-type assumptions, overcome the curse of dimensionality in that computational cost grows at most polynomially in the dimension and
1/\varepsilon
,
where
\varepsilon
is the prescribed accuracy.
Earlier works focus on semilinear PDEs in which the nonlinearity does not depend on the gradient.
Our work is more closely related to recent works that address gradient-dependent nonlinearities,
including
[
65
,
63
,
85
,
87
]
.
Compared to these latter works, a major challenge for us is addressing the discontinuities introduced by the reflection mechanism;
see, e.g., discussion in Section
1
. This requires us to delve deeply into the subject of derivative processes
for reflected diffusions on polyhedral domains
[
78
,
79
,
80
]
, a topic of substantial interest in its own right,
derive new results on these processes, and establish key building blocks in the analysis of the MLP scheme using new techniques,
such as the Bismut-Elworthy-Li type formula for reflected diffusions and properties of a contractive functional operator whose fixed point
characterizes the value function and its gradient. Through these innovations, we extend the MLP framework to
include sample complexity guarantees for semilinear HJB equations with oblique derivative boundary conditions,
and, under appropriate conditions, establishes that MLP overcomes the curse of dimensionality.
Finally, one related work that addresses reflections on the boundary in the MLP literature is
[
23
]
.
Different from our work, they focus on normal reflections on a smooth boundary, and they do not consider gradient-dependent nonlinearities.

Reflected diffusions and derivative processes.

The development and analysis of our MLP scheme rely crucially on reflected diffusions and on their sensitivity
with respect to data that define them.
Foundational results on reflected diffusions establish existence and/or uniqueness, where the boundary of the state space may be smooth or non-smooth,
and the directions of reflections may be normal or oblique

[
97
,
98
,
26
,
35
,
36
,
58
,
77
,
96
,
100
,
101
,
102
,
105
]
.
A central object underpinning this theory is the deterministic Skorokhod map,
whose fundamental properties – most notably Lipschitz continuity – have been extensively developed in
[
34
,
37
,
38
,
53
,
93
]
.
Sensitivity properties of reflected diffusions have been analyzed more recently in
[
81
,
78
,
79
,
80
,
1
,
30
]
.
We focus on reflected diffusions in the nonnegative orthant, with oblique reflections on the boundary
[
58
]
,
and, in particular, notions of derivatives with respect to infinitesimal perturbations to the driving data and the initial condition.

[
81
]
proves the existence of directional derivatives of Skorokhod maps in the nonnegative orthant with oblique reflections,
and characterize the types of path discontinuities that arise. Because that approach is not the most convenienct for our setting,
we adopt the axiomatic framework developed in
[
78
,
80
]
,
which characterize derivative processes as right-continuous regularizations of directional derivatives of reflected diffusions.

[
78
,
80
]
further show that if the Skorokhod map satisfies a certain boundary jitter property –
which holds in our problem class – then derivative processes exist and are pathwise unique.
We use properties of derivative processes established in
[
78
,
80
]
,
together with new properties proved in this paper, to analyze our MLP scheme and associated stochastic functional fixed-point equation.
Related developments include
[
30
]
, which studies derivative processes in the orthant with normal reflections,
and
[
1
]
, which extends
[
30
]
to convex polyhedron with oblique reflections, up to the first hitting time to the non-smooth portion of the boundary.

3
Problem Description

Fix a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
with sample space
\Omega
,
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}
, probability measure
\mathbb{P}

and filtration
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}:t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\}
.
Suppose that the probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
is complete, and the filtration
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}
satisfies the usual conditions
in the sense of e.g., Definition 1.2.25 in Karatzas and Shreve (1998);
that is,
\mathcal{F}_{0}
contains all
\mathbb{P}
-null sets in
\mathcal{F}
, and
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}:t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\}
is right-continuous.
Let
B=\{B(t),t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\}
be a standard
d
-dimensional
\{{\cal F}_{t}\}
-adapted Brownian motion defined on the probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
.

Let
T\in(0,\infty)
. Given the problem dimension
d\in\mathbb{N}
,
we consider a multi-dimensional drift rate stochastic control problem with state space
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
over a finite time horizon
[0,T]
.
The state
Z(\cdot)
evolves according to Eq. (
2
):

Z(t)=Z(0)+\int_{0}^{t}b\bigl(Z(s),a(s)\bigr)\,ds+\sigma B(t)+RY(t).

Here, the initial state is
Z(0)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
, the control process
a(\cdot)
is a
K
-dimensional
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}
-adapted process
with action space
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, so that for all
t\geq 0
,
a\in\mathcal{A}
,
the function
b:\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{K}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the drift coefficient,
the matrix
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is the constant diffusion coefficient,
the matrix
R\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is the so-called reflection matrix,
and
Y=\{Y(t):t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\}
is a
d
-dimensional
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}
-adapted regulator process that ensures a.s.
Z(t)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
;
we describe the process
Y
in more detail in the sequel.
Here, though we do not explicit spell out the dependence of the problem data (
b
,
\sigma
,
R
, etc) on
d
,
we are implicitly considering a sequence of drift control problems with reflections, indexed by the problem dimension
d
.
The following conditions are assumed on
b
,
\mathcal{A}
,
\sigma
,
R
and
Y
, respectively, throughout the paper.

Assumption 3.1
.

The action space
\mathcal{A}
is a compact subset of
\mathbb{R}^{K}
. Furthermore,
there exists a constant
C_{\mathcal{A}}>0
, independent of the dimension
K
, such that for all
a\in\mathcal{A}
,

\|a\|_{\infty}\leq C_{\mathcal{A}}.

(16)

Assumption 3.2
.

There exists a constant
C_{b}>0
, independent of
d
, such that

\|b(x,a)\|_{\infty}\leq C_{b},\quad\text{ for all }(x,a)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}\times\mathcal{A},

(17)

and for all
x,y
and
a\in\mathcal{A}
,

\|b(x,a)-b(y,a)\|_{\infty}\leq C_{b}\|x-y\|_{\infty}.

(18)

Assumption 3.3
.

The covariance matrix
\sigma\sigma^{\top}
is uniformly elliptic in the following sense.
There exists a constant
C_{\sigma}>0
,
independent of
d
, such that for all vectors
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

C_{\sigma}^{-1}\|y\|_{2}^{2}\leq y^{\top}\sigma\sigma^{\top}y\leq C_{\sigma}\|y\|_{2}^{2}.

In other words,
\|\sigma\|_{2}\leq C_{\sigma}
and
\|\sigma^{-1}\|_{2}\leq C_{\sigma}
.

Assumption 3.4
.

The reflection matrix
R
takes the following form.

R=I-Q^{\top}
, where
I
is the
d\times d
identity matrix,
and
Q=(q_{ij})_{i,j=1}^{d}
is a nonnegative
d\times d
matrix with zero diagonal entries and all row sums being no larger than
1
.
In other words,
q_{ii}=0
and
\sum_{j=1}^{d}q_{ij}\leq 1
for all
i=1,\dots,d
.
Furthermore, we assume the following uniform contraction condition on
Q
(cf.
[
21
]
): There exists
\delta\in(0,1)

and
\alpha_{0}\in(0,\infty)
, both of which are independent of
d
, such that

\|\bm{1}^{\top}Q^{n}\|_{\infty}\leq\alpha_{0}(1-\delta)^{n},\quad n\geq 1.

(19)

The state process
Z(\cdot)
is constrained to stay within the nonnegative orthant
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
via the regulator process
Y
,
where, for each
j=1,\cdots,d
,
Y_{j}=\{Y_{j}(t):t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\}
is a non-decreasing process with
Y_{j}(0)=0
, and
Y_{j}
only increases when
Z_{j}
is zero, i.e.,

\int_{0}^{T}\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z_{j}(t)>0\}}\,dY_{j}(t)=0
.
In other words, under a control process
a(\cdot)
, if
Z
solves the reflected stochastic differential equation (
2
),
and if we define the process
X
by

X(t)=Z(0)+\int_{0}^{t}b\bigl(Z(s),a(s)\bigr)\,ds+\sigma B(t),

(20)

then
(Z,Y)
solves the Skorokhod problem for
X
(cf. Section
4.1
).

We are interested in minimizing the total expected discounted cost incurred over the time horizon
[0,T]
.
More specifically, let
\beta\geq 0
be the discount rate.
Let
c:\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}\times\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
be the running cost function, and let

\xi:\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
be the terminal cost function.
Furthermore, for each
j=1,\cdots,d
, whenever the regulator process
Y_{j}
needs to increase to keep
Z_{j}
nonnegative, a “pushing” penalty is incurred at rate
\kappa_{j}\geq 0
.

Let
t\in[0,T]
.
Under a control process
a(\cdot)
, the total expected discounted cost-to-go incurred from time
t
to
T
,
conditioned on
Z(t)=x
, is

J(t,x,a):=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}e^{-\beta(s-t)}\,c\bigl(Z(s),a(s)\bigr)\,ds+\int_{t}^{T}e^{-\beta(s-t)}\,\kappa^{\top}dY(s)+e^{-\beta(T-t)}\,\xi(Z(T))\Big|Z(t)=x\right].

(21)

Define the value function
V:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
by

V(t,x):=\inf_{a(\cdot)}J(t,x,a).

(22)

Our goal is to efficiently compute the value function
V(t,x)
for given
(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
.

To ensure that the cost-to-go and value functions are well-defined, we assume that the cost functions
c
and
\xi
satisfy certain growth conditions.

Assumption 3.5
.

Both the running cost function
c
and the terminal cost function
\xi
are continuously differentiable, i.e.,
c\in C^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}\times{\cal A})
, and
\xi\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d})
.
Furthermore,
c
and
\xi
satisfy the following polynomial growth condition:
There exists positive constants
\alpha_{w}
and
\alpha_{0}\geq 1
,
both of which are independent from the problem dimension
d
,
such that for all
x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
and
a\in{\cal A}
,

|c(x,a)|\leq\alpha_{w}\left(1+(\log d)^{\alpha_{0}/2}+\|x\|_{\infty}^{\alpha_{0}}\right),\text{ and }|\xi(x)|\leq\alpha_{w}\left(1+(\log d)^{\alpha_{0}/2}+\|x\|_{\infty}^{\alpha_{0}}\right).

(23)

Let
\alpha_{w}
and
\alpha_{0}
be as in Assumption
3.5
.
Define the weight function
w:\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
by

w(x)=1+(\log d)^{\alpha_{0}/2}+\|x\|_{\infty}^{\alpha_{0}}.

(24)

Then, under Assumption
3.5
, for all
(x,a)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}\times{\cal A}
, we have

|c(x,a)|\leq\alpha_{w}w(x),\text{ and }|\xi(x)|\leq\alpha_{w}w(x).

(25)

Let us briefly remark that if the cost functions
c
and/or
\xi
do not initially satisfy Assumption
3.5
, they can often be scaled to satisfy the assumption.
For example, if
c(x,a)=h^{\top}x
is a linear function of
x
, with
\|h\|_{\infty}
being uniformly bounded in
d
, we can scale
c(x,a)

by a factor of
1/d
so it satisfies the assumption.

4
Technical Preliminaries

In this section, we introduce reflected diffusions and their associated derivative processes,
and recall some useful properties established in prior works
[
78
,
79
,
80
]
.
To do so, we need to introduce the deterministic Skorokhod problem (Section
4.1
),
the concept of directional derivatives (Section
4.2
) and
the derivative problem (Section
4.3
).
We also prove some new results, specifically, Proposition
4.16
, Corollary
4.17
, Lemma
4.24
,
Proposition
4.25
, and Corollary
4.27
,
which are useful in later sections.
For readability, their proofs are deferred to Appendix
A
.
Finally, our last subsection here recalls the definition of viscosity solution for the parabolic HJB equation (
4
) – (
6
).

4.1
Skorokhod Problem

Definition 4.1

(Skorokhod Problem)
.

Given
f\in\mathbb{C}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and reflection matrix
R
, the pair of functions
(h,g)\in\mathbb{C}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d})\times\mathbb{C}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d})

solves the
Skorokhod problem
(associated with
R
) for input
f
, if the following properties hold:

(i)

h(t)=f(t)+Rg(t)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
for all
t\geq 0
;

(ii)

For each
j=1,\cdots,d
,
g_{j}
is a non-decreasing function with
g_{j}(0)=0
;

(iii)

g_{j}
increases only when
h_{j}
is zero, i.e.

\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbbm{1}_{\{h_{j}(t)>0\}}\,dg_{j}(t)=0,j=1,\cdots,d.

The seminal paper
[
58
]
establishes that for any
f\in\mathbb{C}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
there exists a unique solution
(h,g)
to the Skorokhod problem for
f
.
Thus, the Skorokhod map
\Gamma:\mathbb{C}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{C}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d})
defined by

\Gamma:f\mapsto h
is well-defined.
It is also well known that the Skorokhod map
\Gamma
is Lipschitz continuous
[
22
,
58
,
93
,
34
,
37
,
38
]
.
Furthermore, by Lemma 3 of
[
21
]
, under Assumption
3.4
, the Lipschitz constant does not grow with
d
.

Proposition 4.2

(Lemma 3 of
[
21
]
)
.

There exists a constant
C_{\Gamma}>0
, which is independent of the problem dimension
d
,
such that if
(h_{i},g_{i})
is a solution to the Skorokhod problem for
f_{i}\in\mathbb{C}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
i=1,2
, then for all
t\in[0,\infty)
,

\|h_{1}-h_{2}\|_{t}+\|g_{1}-g_{2}\|_{t}\leq C_{\Gamma}\|f_{1}-f_{2}\|_{t}.

4.2
Directional Derivatives

For
f\in\mathbb{C}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\psi\in\mathbb{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and
\varepsilon>0
, define

\nabla_{\psi}^{\varepsilon}\Gamma(f):=\frac{\Gamma(f+\varepsilon\psi)-\Gamma(f)}{\varepsilon}.

(26)

Here, it is assumed that
\nabla_{\psi}^{\varepsilon}\Gamma(f)
is only defined for
f
,
\psi
and
\varepsilon>0
such that

f+\varepsilon\psi\in\mathbb{C}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, i.e.,
f(0)+\varepsilon\psi(0)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
.

Definition 4.3
.

Given
f\in\mathbb{C}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\psi\in\mathbb{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
the
directional derivative
of
\Gamma
evaluated at
f
in the direction
\psi
is a function

\nabla_{\psi}\Gamma(f):\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}^{d},

defined as the pointwise limit

\nabla_{\psi}\Gamma(f)(t):=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\nabla_{\psi}^{\varepsilon}\Gamma(f)(t),\qquad t\geq 0.

Proposition 4.4

(Proposition 2.17 in
[
78
]
)
.

Given
f\in\mathbb{C}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\psi\in\mathbb{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that

\nabla_{\psi}\Gamma(f)
exists, suppose that
\{\psi_{\varepsilon}\}_{\varepsilon>0}
is a family of functions in
\mathbb{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that

\psi_{\varepsilon}\to\psi
in
\mathbb{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
under the uniform topology, as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
. Then,

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\nabla^{\varepsilon}_{\psi_{\varepsilon}}\Gamma(f)(t)=\nabla_{\psi}\Gamma(f)(t),\quad t\geq 0.

(27)

Proposition 4.5

(Proposition 6.3 in
[
78
]
)
.

Given
f\in\mathbb{C}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\in\mathbb{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
suppose that both
\nabla_{\psi_{1}}\Gamma(f)
and
\nabla_{\psi_{2}}\Gamma(f)
exist.
Then for all
t\in[0,\infty)
,

\|\nabla_{\psi_{1}}\Gamma(f)-\nabla_{\psi_{2}}\Gamma(f)\|_{t}\leq C_{\Gamma}\|\psi_{1}-\psi_{2}\|_{t},

(28)

where we recall that
C_{\Gamma}
is the Lipschitz constant of the Skorokhod map
\Gamma

from Proposition
4.2
.

4.3
The Derivative Problem

Denote the
j
th column vector of the reflection matrix
R
by
R_{j}
; this is also a direction of reflection on the face
\{x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}:x_{j}=0\}
.
For
x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
, define the set
{\cal I}(x)
of indices
j
for which
x_{j}=0
by

{\cal I}(x):=\left\{j=1,2,\cdots,d:x_{j}=0\right\}.

(29)

In particular, if
x_{j}>0
for all
j
, in which case we simply write
x>0
, then
{\cal I}(x)=\emptyset
.
For
x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
, also define the set
R(x)
of reflection directions at
x
by

R(x):=\{R_{j}:j\in{\cal I}(x)\}.

(30)

For
x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
, if
{\cal I}(x)\neq\emptyset
, i.e., if
x\in\partial\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
lies on the boundary of
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
,
define the subspace
H_{x}
by

H_{x}:=\left\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:y_{j}=0\text{ for all }j\in{\cal I}(x)\right\},

(31)

and the set
G_{x}
of allowed directions of perturbation by

G_{x}:=\left\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:y_{j}\geq 0\text{ for all }j\in{\cal I}(x)\right\}.

(32)

If
{\cal I}(x)=\emptyset
, i.e., if
x>0
, set
H_{x}:=\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
G_{x}:=\mathbb{R}^{d}
. As explained in, e.g.,
[
80
]
,
if
x\in\partial\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
, then for
y\in H_{x}
,
{\cal I}(x+\varepsilon y)={\cal I}(x)
for sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
,
and for
y\in G_{x}
,
x+\varepsilon y\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
for sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
.

Definition 4.6
.

Let
f\in\mathbb{C}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and suppose that
(h,g)
solves the Skorokhod problem associated with
R
for
f
.
Let
\psi\in\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then
(\phi,\eta)\in\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a solution to the
derivative problem
along
h
for
\psi
if

\eta(0)\in\text{span}\left[R(h(0))\right]
, and if, for all
t\geq 0
, the following conditions hold:

(i)

\phi(t)=\psi(t)+\eta(t)
for all
t\geq 0
;

(ii)

\phi(t)\in H_{h(t)}
for all
t\geq 0
;

(iii)

for all
0\leq s<t
,

\eta(t)-\eta(s)\in\text{span}\left[\bigcup_{r\in(s,t]}R(h(r))\right].

If there exists a unique solution
(\phi,\eta)
to the derivative problem along
h
for
\psi
, we write
\phi=\Lambda_{h}[\psi]
,
and refer to
\Lambda_{h}
as the derivative map along
h
.

Lemma 4.7

(Lemma 5.1 in
[
78
]
)
.

Suppose
(\phi_{i},\eta_{i})
solves the derivative problem along
h
for
\psi_{i}\in\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
i=1,2
, respectively.
Then, for all
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
, the pair

(\alpha_{1}\phi_{1}+\alpha_{2}\phi_{2},\;\alpha_{1}\eta_{1}+\alpha_{2}\eta_{2})

solves the derivative problem along
h
for
\alpha_{1}\psi_{1}+\alpha_{2}\psi_{2}
.

Lemma 4.8

(Lemma 5.2 in
[
78
]
)
.

Let
f\in\mathbb{C}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and suppose that
(h,g)
solves the Skorokhod problem for
f
.
Let
\psi\in\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and suppose
(\phi,\eta)\in\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
solves the derivative problem along
h
for
\psi
.
Define
h^{s}
,
\phi^{s}
,
\psi^{s}
, and
\eta^{s}
by

h^{s}(\cdot):=h(s+\cdot),\phi^{s}(\cdot):=\phi(s+\cdot),\psi^{s}(\cdot):=\phi(s)+\psi(s+\cdot)-\psi(s),\eta^{s}(\cdot):=\eta(s+\cdot)-\eta(s).

Then,
(\phi^{s},\eta^{s})
solves the derivative problem along
h^{s}
for
\psi^{s}
.

Proposition 4.9

(Theorem 5.4 of
[
78
]
)
.

There exists a constant
C_{\Lambda}>0
, which is independent of the problem dimension
d
,
such that if
(h,g)
solves the Skorokhod problem for
f
,
and
(\phi_{i},\eta_{i})
solves the derivative problem along
h
for
\psi_{i}\in\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
i=1,2
,
then for all
t\in(0,\infty)
,

\|\phi_{1}-\phi_{2}\|_{t}\leq C_{\Lambda}\|\psi_{1}-\psi_{2}\|_{t}.

Furthermore, the constant
C_{\Lambda}
does not depend on
h\in\mathbb{C}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d})
.

Remark 4.10
.

Theorem 5.4 of
[
78
]
does not directly establish growth rate of
C_{\Lambda}

in the problem dimension
d
, but an inspection of its proof shows that under Assumption
3.4
,
C_{\Lambda}
does not grow with
d
.

The following proposition is one of the main results established in
[
78
]
,
specialized to the more restrictive setting of this paper.
It provides a sharp characterization of the relationship between the derivative problem and the notion of directional derivative.

Proposition 4.11

(Theorem 3.11 of
[
78
]
)
.

Given
f\in\mathbb{C}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, suppose that
(h,g)
solves the Skorokhod problem for
f
.
Then for all
\psi\in\mathbb{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\nabla_{\psi}\Gamma(f)
exists, lies in
\mathbb{D}_{\ell,r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
and
\Lambda_{h}[\psi]
is equal to the right-continuous regularization of
\nabla_{\psi}\Gamma(f)
; i.e.,

\Lambda_{h}[\psi](t)=\nabla_{\psi}\Gamma(f)(t+)\text{ for all }t\geq 0.

4.4
Reflected Diffusions

Let
\tilde{b}:\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a continuously differentiable function that satisfies the following assumption.

Assumption 4.12
.

There exists a constant
C_{\tilde{b}}\in(0,\infty)
and
\alpha_{\tilde{b}}\in(0,1]
, both of which are independent of
d
, such that

\|D\tilde{b}(x)\|_{\infty}\leq C_{\tilde{b}}
and

\|\tilde{b}(x)\|_{\infty}\leq C_{\tilde{b}}
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
,
and for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
,
\|D\tilde{b}(x)-D\tilde{b}(y)\|_{\infty}\leq C_{\tilde{b}}\|x-y\|^{\alpha_{\tilde{b}}}_{\infty}.

Definition 4.13

(Reflected diffusion)
.

Given drift coefficient function
\tilde{b}(\cdot)
, initial condition
x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
, reflection matrix
R
, and the Brownian motion
B(\cdot)
,
the associated reflected diffusion is a
d
-dimensional continuous
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}
-adapted process
\tilde{Z}^{x}=\{\tilde{Z}^{x}(t):t\geq 0\}
such that a.s.,

\tilde{Z}^{x}(t)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
for all
t\geq 0
,
and

\tilde{Z}^{x}(t)=x+\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{b}\bigl(\tilde{Z}^{x}(s)\bigr)\,ds+\sigma B(t)+R\tilde{Y}^{x}(t),

(33)

where
\tilde{Y}^{x}=\{\tilde{Y}^{x}(t):t\geq 0\}
is a
d
-dimensional continuous
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}
-adapted process such that a.s., for each
j=1,\cdots,d
,

\tilde{Y}_{j}^{x}(0)=0
, the process
\tilde{Y}_{j}^{x}(\cdot)
is non-decreasing, and

\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tilde{Z}_{j}^{x}(s)>0\}}\,d\tilde{Y}_{j}^{x}(s)=0.

Denote the “free” part of the process
\tilde{Z}^{x}
by
\tilde{X}^{x}
. More precisely,

\tilde{X}^{x}(t)=x+\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{b}\bigl(\tilde{Z}^{x}(s)\bigr)\,ds+\sigma B(t),\quad t\geq 0,

(34)

and
\tilde{Z}^{x}=\Gamma\left(\tilde{X}^{x}\right)
.
The following result is well-known (see, e.g., Theorem 4.3 of
[
93
]
).

Proposition 4.14
.

Suppose
\tilde{b}(\cdot)
,
\sigma
and
R
satisfy Assumptions
4.12
,
3.3
and
3.4
.
Then, for each
x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
and
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}
-Brownian motion
B(\cdot)
, there exists an associated reflected diffusion
\tilde{Z}^{x}
, and
\tilde{Z}^{x}
is a strong Markov process. Furthermore, if
\bar{Z}^{x}
is another such reflected diffusion, then a.s.
\tilde{Z}^{x}=\bar{Z}^{x}
. In other words, pathwise uniqueness holds.

Lemma 4.15

(Lemma 4.13 of
[
80
]
)
.

Suppose Assumption
3.3
holds. Then,

\mathbb{P}\left(\tilde{Z}^{x}(t)>0\right)=1
for all
t>0
.

We establish some moment bounds on
\tilde{Z}^{x}
in the following proposition.

Proposition 4.16
.

Let
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}
. Then for all
t\geq 0
and
\alpha\geq 1
, there exists a constant
C=C(t,\alpha,C_{\sigma},C_{\tilde{b}},C_{\Gamma})
that only depends on

t
,
\alpha
,
C_{\sigma}
,
C_{\tilde{b}}
and
C_{\Gamma}
, such that

\mathbb{E}\left[\|\tilde{Z}^{x}\|_{t}^{\alpha}\right]\leq C\left(1+(\log d)^{\alpha/2}+\|x\|^{\alpha}_{\infty}\right).

(35)

Furthermore, for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
, a.s.

\|\tilde{Z}^{y}-\tilde{Z}^{x}\|_{t}\leq C\|y-x\|_{\infty}.

(36)

The following corollary is a simple but useful consequence of the moment bounds on
\tilde{Z}^{x}
in Proposition
4.16
.
Recall the definition of the weight function
w
in Eq. (
24
).

Corollary 4.17
.

Let
\alpha\geq 1
, and let
T>0
. Then, there exists a positive constant
C_{\alpha,T}
that depends only on
\alpha
and
T

(as well as
C_{\sigma}
,
C_{\tilde{b}}
and
C_{\Gamma}
) but not on
d
, such that
for all
t\in[0,T]
, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
,

\mathbb{E}\left[w^{\alpha}\left(\tilde{Z}^{x}(t)\right)\right]\leq C_{\alpha,T}w^{\alpha}(x).

(37)

4.5
Derivative Processes

Definition 4.18

(Derivative process)
.

Let
x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
, let
B(\cdot)
be a
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}
-Brownian motion, and let
\tilde{Z}^{x}
be an associated reflected diffusion.
A
derivative process along
\tilde{Z}^{x}
is an RCLL
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}
-adapted process
D\tilde{Z}^{x}=\{D\tilde{Z}^{x}(t):t\geq 0\}
taking values in
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
such that a.s., for all
t\geq 0
,

D_{j}\tilde{Z}^{x}(t)\in H_{\tilde{Z}^{x}(t)}\text{ for }j=1,\dots,d,

where
D_{j}\tilde{Z}^{x}(t)
denotes the
j
th column of
D\tilde{Z}^{x}(t)
,
and
D\tilde{Z}^{x}
satisfies

D\tilde{Z}^{x}(t)=I+\int_{0}^{t}D\tilde{b}\bigl(\tilde{Z}^{x}(s)\bigr)D\tilde{Z}^{x}(s)\,ds+\tilde{\eta}(t),

where
\tilde{\eta}=\{\tilde{\eta}(t):t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\}
is an RCLL
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}
-adapted process taking values in
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
such that a.s.
\tilde{\eta}_{j}(0)\in\text{span}[R(x)]
,
j=1,2,\cdots,d
, and for all
0\leq s<t<\infty
,

\tilde{\eta}_{j}(t)-\tilde{\eta}_{j}(s)\in\text{span}\left[\bigcup_{r\in(s,t]}R\bigl(\tilde{Z}^{x}(r)\bigr)\right].

In addition to
D\tilde{Z}^{x}
, we also define a
derivative process along
\tilde{Y}^{x}
as the process
R^{-1}\tilde{\eta}
, which we denote by
D\tilde{Y}^{x}
.

Remark 4.19
.

Besides the definition of the standard derivative process
D\tilde{Z}^{x}
in
[
80
]
,
we also define the derivative process
D\tilde{Y}^{x}
, which is a regularized version of the pathwise derivative of

\tilde{Y}^{x}
with respect to
x
; see Proposition
4.21
in the sequel. The process
D\tilde{Y}^{x}
is useful in several ways;
for example, an important use of
D\tilde{Y}^{x}
is in characterizing the derivative of

\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{T}e^{-\beta s}\kappa^{\top}d\tilde{Y}^{x}(s)\right],

the total expected pushing penalty cost, with respect to the initial condition
x
.

Proposition 4.20

(Theorem 3.6 in
[
80
]
)
.

Let
x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
and
B(\cdot)
be as above. Then pathwise uniqueness holds for the derivative process
D\tilde{Z}^{x}
along
\tilde{Z}^{x}
,
and, as an easy consequence, also for the derivative process
D\tilde{Y}^{x}
along
\tilde{Y}^{x}
.

The following proposition is the main result in
[
80
]
, specialized to the more restrictive setting of this paper.

Proposition 4.21

(Theorem 3.13 in
[
80
]
)
.

Suppose Assumptions
4.12
,
3.3
and
3.4
are in place.
Let
x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
. Then, there exists a pathwise unique derivative process
D\tilde{Z}^{x}
along
\tilde{Z}^{x}
, and for all
y\in G_{x}
, a.s. the following hold.

(i)

The pathwise derivative of
\tilde{Z}^{x}
in the direction
y
, defined by

\partial_{y}\tilde{Z}^{x}(t):=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{\tilde{Z}^{x+\varepsilon y}(t)-\tilde{Z}^{x}(t)}{\varepsilon},t\geq 0,

exists. The pathwise derivative of
\tilde{Y}^{x}
in the direction
y
, defined for all
t\geq 0
by

\partial_{y}\tilde{Y}^{x}(t):=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{\tilde{Y}^{x+\varepsilon y}(t)-\tilde{Y}^{x}(t)}{\varepsilon},

also exists.

(ii)

The pathwise derivatives
\partial_{y}\tilde{Z}^{x}=\{\partial_{y}\tilde{Z}^{x}(t):t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\}
and

\partial_{y}\tilde{Y}^{x}=\{\partial_{y}\tilde{Y}^{x}(t):t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\}
take values in
\mathbb{D}_{\ell,r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and are continuous at times
t>0
when
\tilde{Z}^{x}(t)>0
, or when
\tilde{Z}^{x}_{j}=0
for more than one
j\in\{1,2,\cdots,d\}
.

(iii)

The right-continuous regularization of the pathwise derivative
\partial_{y}\tilde{Z}^{x}
(
\partial_{y}\tilde{Y}^{x}
, respectively) is equal to the derivative process
D\tilde{Z}^{x}

(
D\tilde{Y}^{x}
, respectively) evaluated in the direction
y
, i.e.

\lim_{s\downarrow t}\partial_{y}\tilde{Z}^{x}(s)=D\tilde{Z}^{x}(t)y,\ \text{ and }\ \lim_{s\downarrow t}\partial_{y}\tilde{Y}^{x}(s)=D\tilde{Y}^{x}(t)y,\qquad t\geq 0.

Remark 4.22
.

(a) In part (iii) of Proposition
4.21
, both
D\tilde{Z}^{x}(t)y
and
D\tilde{Y}^{x}(t)y
are simply matrix-vector multiplications.
We also denote these quantities as
D\tilde{Z}^{x}(t;y)
and
D\tilde{Y}^{x}(t;y)
, respectively, to emphasize their dependences on
y
,
the direction in which derivatives are taken.

(b) Let us note the following simple but useful observation. For all
j=1,2,\cdots,d
, the
j
th standard unit vector
e_{j}\in G_{x}

for any
x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
, i.e.,
e_{j}
is always a valid direction in which pathwise derivatives can be taken.
Then,

D\tilde{Z}^{x}(t;e_{j})=D\tilde{Z}^{x}(t)e_{j}=D_{j}\tilde{Z}^{x}(t),\text{ and }D\tilde{Y}^{x}(t;e_{j})=D_{j}\tilde{Y}^{x}(t),

where
D_{j}\tilde{Z}^{x}(t)
(
D_{j}\tilde{Y}^{x}(t)
, respectively) denotes the
j
th column of the matrix
D\tilde{Z}^{x}(t)
(
D\tilde{Y}^{x}(t)
, respectively).
In other words, the
j
th column of
D\tilde{Z}^{x}(t)
(
D\tilde{Y}^{x}(t)
, respectively) is the regularized
j
th partial derivative of
\tilde{Z}^{x}(t)
(
\tilde{Y}^{x}(t)
, respectively) in
x
.

Proposition 4.23

(Theorem 4 of
[
79
]
)
.

Let
\{f_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence in
\mathbb{C}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
that converges to
f\in\mathbb{C}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
as
n\to\infty
.
Let
(h,g)
solve the Skorokhod problem for
f
, and for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
(h_{n},g_{n})
solve the Skorokhod problem for
f_{n}
.
Let
\psi\in\mathbb{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\psi(0)\in H_{h(0)}
and
\{\psi_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence in
\mathbb{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that

\psi_{n}\to\psi
in
\mathbb{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
as
n\to\infty
. Then,

\Lambda_{h_{n}}(\psi_{n})\to\Lambda_{h}(\psi)\ \ \text{in }\ \mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\ \text{as }\ n\to\infty.

We derive a simple bound for the derivative process
D\tilde{Z}^{x}
in the following lemma.

Lemma 4.24
.

Let
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}
, and let
j\in\{1,2,\cdots,d\}
. There exists a positive constant
C
, independent from
x
and
j
, such that a.s.

\|D_{j}\tilde{Z}^{x}\|_{t}:=\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\|D_{j}\tilde{Z}^{x}(s)\|_{\infty}\leq C.

Furthermore,
C=C(t,C_{\Lambda},C_{\tilde{b}})
depends only on
t
,
C_{\Lambda}
and
C_{\tilde{b}}
, but not on the problem dimension
d
.

The next result establishes the continuity of derivative processes
D\tilde{Z}^{x}
in the initial state
x\in\mathbb{R}_{++}^{d}
,
in an appropriate sense. Its proof, deferred to Appendix
A
, is an application of Proposition
4.23
.

Proposition 4.25
.

Let
x\in\mathbb{R}_{++}^{d}
and
(y^{(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a fixed sequence in
\mathbb{R}_{++}^{d}
with
y^{(n)}\to x
as
n\to\infty
.
Let
D\tilde{Z}^{x}
be the derivative process along
\tilde{Z}^{x}
, and for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
let
D\tilde{Z}^{y^{(n)}}
be the derivative process along
\tilde{Z}^{y^{(n)}}
.
Then a.s. for each
j
,

D_{j}\tilde{Z}^{y^{(n)}}\to D_{j}\tilde{Z}^{x}\ \text{in }\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\text{ as }n\to\infty.

Remark 4.26
.

Proposition
4.25
shows the continuity of the derivative process with respect to the initial condition
x
,
as long as
x
is in the interior of the nonnegative orthant, i.e.,
x\in\mathbb{R}_{++}^{d}
.
This is, in some sense, best possible; see also Proposition
4.23
(Theorem 4 in
[
79
]

and the ensuing discussion). Indeed, for a one-dimensional reflected Brownian motion with initial condition
x
,
let
\tau_{x}
be its first hitting time to zero.
Then, at
x=0
, the derivative process is everywhere zero, and for
x>0
, the derivative process is
1
before
\tau_{x}
,
and zero after. It can be verified that the derivative process at
x>0
does not converge to that at
x=0
in the Skorokhod topology.

Corollary 4.27
.

Let
j\in\{1,2,\cdots,d\}
, and let
(y^{(n)})
,
x
, and
D_{j}\tilde{Z}^{y^{(n)}}
and
D_{j}\tilde{Z}^{x}
be the same as in Proposition
4.25
.
In particular,
x\in\mathbb{R}_{++}^{d}
.
Define the continuous martingales

I(y^{(n)}),n\in\mathbb{N},
and
I(x)
, by

I(y^{(n)})(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\left[\sigma^{-1}D_{j}\tilde{Z}^{y^{(n)}}(s)\right]^{\top}\,dB(s),\ \text{ and }\ I(x)(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\left[\sigma^{-1}D_{j}\tilde{Z}^{x}(s)\right]^{\top}\,dB(s).

Then,
\mathbb{E}\left[\|I(y^{(n)})-I(x)\|_{t}^{2}\right]\to 0\text{ as }n\to\infty

for each
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

4.6
Viscosity Solution

Recall the parabolic HJB equation with oblique boundary conditions in Eqs. (
4
) – (
6
), reproduced here for convenience:

V_{t}(t,x)+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{Tr}\left(\sigma\sigma^{\top}D_{xx}V(t,x)\right)+\tilde{b}(x)^{\top}D_{x}V(t,x)+\tilde{\cal H}\left(x,D_{x}V(t,x)\right)-\beta V(t,x)=0,

where

{\cal H}(x,p):=\inf_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\left\{(b(x,a)^{\top}p+c(x,a)\right\}
for
(x,p)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}.

The terminal condition is given by

V(T,x)=\xi(x),\text{ for all}\ x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+},

and the oblique boundary conditions are

\langle R_{i},D_{x}V(t,x)\rangle=-\kappa_{i},\text{ if }x_{i}=0,t<T.

Define
\Phi(t,x,r,q,p,X)
by

\displaystyle\Phi(t,x,r,p,q,X)=-q-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{Tr}(\sigma\sigma^{\top}X)-{\cal H}(x,p)+\beta r.

(38)

Define also
\Phi_{*}(t,x,r,q,p,X)
by

\displaystyle\Phi_{*}(t,x,r,q,p,X)=\begin{cases}\Phi(t,x,r,q,p,X),&\text{if }x>0;\\
\Phi(t,x,r,q,p,X)\wedge\min_{i\in\mathcal{I}(x)}\left\{-R_{i}^{\top}p-\kappa_{i}\right\},&\text{if }x\in\partial\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d},\end{cases}

(39)

and
\Phi^{*}(t,x,r,q,p,X)
by

\displaystyle\Phi^{*}(t,x,r,q,p,X)=\begin{cases}\Phi(t,x,r,q,p,X),&\text{if }x>0\\
\Phi(t,x,r,q,p,X)\vee\max_{i\in\mathcal{I}(x)}\left\{-R_{i}^{\top}p-\kappa_{i}\right\},&\text{if }x\in\partial\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}.\end{cases}

(40)

Definition 4.28
.

u\in C([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d})
is a
viscosity solution
of the HJB equation (
4
) – (
6
),
if
u
satisfies the terminal boundary condition Eq. (
6
), and
if for all
\varphi\in C^{1,2}((0,T)\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d})
and
(t,x)\in(0,T)\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
such that
(t,x)
is a strict maximum of
u-\varphi
,

\Phi_{*}(t,x,u(t,x),\varphi_{t}(t,x),\varphi_{x}(t,x),\varphi_{xx}(t,x))\leq 0,

(41)

and for all
\varphi\in C^{1,2}((0,T)\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d})
and

(t,x)\in(0,T)\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
such that
(t,x)
is a strict minimum of
u-\varphi
,

\Phi^{*}(t,x,u(t,x),\varphi_{t}(t,x),\varphi_{x}(t,x),\varphi_{xx}(t,x))\geq 0.

(42)

5
Stochastic Fixed-Point Equation

In this section, we provide a fix-point characterization of the value function gradient.
To do so, we first rigorously define the functional map
F
, formally introduced in Eq. (
14
),
and establish several useful properties.
We then proceed to prove the fixed-point characterization in Theorem
5.5
, our main result of the section.

5.1
Properties of the Functional Map
F

All proofs of the results in this subsection are deferred to Appendix
E
.
Let
T>0
, and let
\mathcal{B}
be the space of measurable functions

v:[0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
with

\sup_{(t,x)\in[0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}}\frac{(T-t)^{1/2}\|v(t,x)\|_{\infty}}{w(x)}<\infty,

and
w:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
defined in Eq. (
24
).

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
\rho\in\mathbb{R}
. Denote

\|v\|_{\rho}:=\sup_{(t,x)\in[0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}}\frac{e^{\rho t}(T-t)^{1/2}\|v(t,x)\|_{\infty}}{w(x)}.

(43)

Then, for all
v\in\mathcal{B}
,
\|v\|_{\rho}<\infty
.
Furthermore,
\|\cdot\|_{\rho}
defines a norm on the space
\mathcal{B}
, and
(\mathcal{B},\|\cdot\|_{\rho})
is a Banach space.

Recall the reference process
\tilde{Z}^{t,x}
defined in Eq. (
11
), i.e.,

\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(s)=x+\int_{t}^{s}\tilde{b}(\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(r))dr+\sigma(B(s)-B(t))+R\tilde{Y}^{t,x}(s),\quad s\in[t,T],

and the functional map
F
formally defined in Eq. (
14
), where
for
(t,x)\in[0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}
,

\displaystyle~F(v)(t,x):=~\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\int_{t}^{T}e^{-\beta(s-t)}\tilde{\cal H}\left(\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(s),v(s,\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(s))\right)\left(\frac{1}{s-t}\int_{t}^{s}\left[\sigma^{-1}D\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(r)\right]^{\top}dB(r)\right)ds

\displaystyle~~~~+\int_{t}^{T}e^{-\beta(s-t)}\kappa^{\top}d\left[D\tilde{Y}^{t,x}(s)\right]+e^{-\beta(T-t)}\xi(\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(T))\left(\frac{1}{T-t}\int_{t}^{T}\left[\sigma^{-1}D\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(r)\right]^{\top}dB(r)\right)\Bigg].

Here, the
Hamiltonian

\tilde{\cal H}:\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is defined by Eq. (
9
), i.e.,
for each
(x,p)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\tilde{\cal H}(x,p):=\inf_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\left\{(b(x,a)-\tilde{b}(x))^{\top}p+c(x,a)\right\}.

Lemma 5.2
.

For
v\in\mathcal{B}
,
\|F(v)\|_{0}<\infty
.

Lemmas
5.2
establishes that
F
is a well-defined map from

(\mathcal{B},\|\cdot\|_{0})
into itself.
By the equivalence of the norms
\|\cdot\|_{\rho}
,
\rho\in\mathbb{R}
,

F
is also a well-defined map from
(\mathcal{B},\|\cdot\|_{\rho})
into itself
for any
\rho\in\mathbb{R}
. The next proposition shows that

F
is contractive for sufficiently large
\rho
.

Proposition 5.3
.

For sufficiently large
\rho\in\mathbb{R}
, the map

F:(\mathcal{B},\|\cdot\|_{\rho})\to(\mathcal{B},\|\cdot\|_{\rho})

where
F
is defined in Eq. (
14
), is contractive.

The following lemma establishes certain continuity-preserving property of the map
F
.

Lemma 5.4
.

For any
v\in\mathcal{B}
such that
v
is continuous on
[0,T)\times\mathbb{R}_{++}^{d}
,
we have that
F(v)
is continuous on
[0,T)\times\mathbb{R}_{++}^{d}
.

5.2
Fixed-Point Characterization

Theorem 5.5
.

(i)

There exists a unique
\tilde{v}\in\mathcal{B}
that is the fixed point of the map
F
,
i.e.,

\tilde{v}=F(\tilde{v}).

(44)

For the rest of the paper, we use
\tilde{v}
to denote the unique fixed point of
F
in
\mathcal{B}
. Then:

(ii)

\tilde{v}
is continuous on
[0,T)\times\mathbb{R}_{++}^{d}
.

(iii)

Define the function
\tilde{V}:[0,T)\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle\tilde{V}(t,x)=

\displaystyle~\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}e^{-\beta(s-t)}\tilde{\cal H}(\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(s),\tilde{v}(s,\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(s)))ds\right.

\displaystyle~+\int_{t}^{T}e^{-\beta(s-t)}\kappa^{\top}d\tilde{Y}^{t,x}(s)+e^{-\beta(T-t)}\xi(\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(T))\Bigg].

(45)

Then
\tilde{V}
is differentiable in
x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
, with

\tilde{v}(t,x)=D_{x}\tilde{V}(t,x),\quad(t,x)\in[0,T)\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}.

(iv)

\tilde{V}
is the unique viscosity solution of the HJB equation (
4
) – (
6
)
that satisfies the polynomial growth condition (
23
) (with
\alpha_{w}
appropriately defined if necessary),
and
\tilde{V}=V
,
where
V
is the value function defined in Eq. (
22
).

Proof.

Part (i) follows immediately from Proposition
5.3
and Banach’s fixed point theorem.
For part (ii), consider the sequence
\{v^{(n)}\}_{n\geq 0}
of functions in
\mathcal{B}
defined recursively as follows.

v^{(0)}\equiv 0
, and
v^{(n)}=F(v^{(n-1)})
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then by Lemma
5.4
, for all
n\geq 0
,
v^{(n)}
is continuous on
[0,T)\times\mathbb{R}_{++}^{d}
.
Furthermore,
v^{(n)}\to\tilde{v}
under norm
\|\cdot\|_{\rho}
, for some sufficiently large
\rho
,
which in turn imply that on any compact subset of
[0,T)\times\mathbb{R}_{++}^{d}
,
the convergence
v^{(n)}\to\tilde{v}
is uniform. Therefore, the limit
\tilde{v}
is continuous on
[0,T)\times\mathbb{R}_{++}^{d}
.
Part (iii) follows by applying the Bismut-Elworthy-Li formula Eq. (
107
) to
\tilde{V}
, noting that

\tilde{\cal H}(x,\tilde{v}(s,x))
and
\xi(x)
are measurable functions of polynomial growth.
Finally, part (iv) follows from the following facts: (a)
\tilde{V}
is a viscosity solution
of the HJB equation (
4
) – (
6
) (Proposition
9.1
);
(b) the value function
V
defined in Eq. (
22
) is also a viscosity solution (Proposition
9.2
);
and (c) the viscosity solution is unique (Proposition
9.3
).
∎

6
Multi-Level Picard Scheme

In this section, we develop the multilevel Picard (MLP) scheme for our drift control problem with reflections,
and establish complexity bounds on the scheme under appropriate assumptions to be detailed.

6.1
The MLP Scheme

Given
(t,x)\in[0,T)\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
, we wish to approximate
\tilde{v}(t,x)
,
as well as
\tilde{V}(t,x)
defined in Eq. (
45
).
We do so by making use of the fact that
\tilde{v}

is the unique fixed point to the functional map
F
introduced in Eq. (
14
), and
that
\tilde{V}
can be computed from
\tilde{v}
.
For notational convenience, we introduce the related functional map
\bar{F}

that relates
\tilde{v}
to
(\tilde{V},\tilde{v})
:
For
v\in{\cal B}
, define
\bar{F}(\bar{v})
by

\displaystyle\bar{F}(v)(t,x)

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}e^{-\beta(s-t)}\tilde{\cal H}(\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(s),\tilde{v}(s,\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(s)))\left(1,\frac{1}{s-t}\int_{t}^{s}\left[\sigma^{-1}D\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(r)\right]^{\top}dB(r)\right)ds\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\left(\int_{t}^{T}e^{-\beta(s-t)}\kappa^{\top}d\tilde{Y}^{t,x}(s),\int_{t}^{T}e^{-\beta(s-t)}\kappa^{\top}d\left[D\tilde{Y}^{t,x}(s)\right]\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\left.+e^{-\beta(T-t)}\xi(\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(T))\left(1,\frac{1}{T-t}\int_{t}^{T}\left[\sigma^{-1}D\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(r)\right]^{\top}dB(r)\right)\right].

(46)

Here, for a vector
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
(1,y)
is the
(d+1)
-dimensional vector that concatenates
1
with
y
.
The fact that
F
is contractive under an appropriate norm
suggests the following natural iterative scheme to approximate
(\tilde{V},\tilde{v})
.
Let

v^{(0)}_{j}(t,x)\equiv 0,j=1,2,\cdots,d,\quad\text{ and }\quad V^{(0)}\equiv 0.

(47)

Recursively define
(V^{(n)},v^{(n)})
by

(V^{(n)},v^{(n)}):=\bar{F}\left(v^{(n-1)}\right),n\in\mathbb{N}.

(48)

We expect
(V^{(n)},v^{(n)})
to approximate
(\tilde{V},\tilde{v})
well for large
n
.
Thus, we now focus on the task of efficiently approximating
(V^{(n)},v^{(n)})
.
First, we rewrite the iteration in Eq. (
48
) as a telescopic sum:

(V^{(n)},v^{(n)})=\bar{F}\left(v^{(0)}\right)+\sum_{\ell=1}^{n-1}\left[\bar{F}\left(v^{(\ell)}\right)-\bar{F}\left(v^{(\ell-1)}\right)\right].

(49)

In particular, for each
j=1,2,\cdots,d
,

v^{(n)}_{j}(t,x)=\left[\bar{F}\left(v^{(0)}\right)\right]_{j+1}(t,x)+\sum_{\ell=1}^{n-1}\left[\bar{F}\left(v^{(\ell)}\right)-\bar{F}\left(v^{(\ell-1)}\right)\right]_{j+1}(t,x),

(50)

and

V^{(n)}(t,x)=\left[\bar{F}\left(v^{(0)}\right)\right]_{1}(t,x)+\sum_{\ell=1}^{n-1}\left[\bar{F}\left(v^{(\ell)}\right)-\bar{F}\left(v^{(\ell-1)}\right)\right]_{1}(t,x).

(51)

Thus, to approximate
(V^{(n)},v^{(n)})(t,x)
, we approximate
\left[\bar{F}\left(v^{(0)}\right)\right](t,x)
and

\left[\bar{F}\left(v^{(\ell)}\right)-\bar{F}\left(v^{(\ell-1)}\right)\right](t,x)
, for
\ell=1,2,\cdots,n-1
, respectively.
Furthermore, because of the contractive property of
F
, we expect that we can use fewer samples
to approximate
\left[\bar{F}\left(v^{(\ell)}\right)-\bar{F}\left(v^{(\ell-1)}\right)\right](t,x)
, for larger
\ell
.
In preparation for the description of the multilevel Picard scheme, we provide a closer examination of the terms

\left[\bar{F}\left(v^{(0)}\right)\right]_{j}(t,x)
and
\left[\bar{F}\left(v^{(\ell)}\right)-\bar{F}\left(v^{(\ell-1)}\right)\right]_{j}(t,x)
,
\ell=1,2,\cdots,n-1
,

j=1,2,\cdots,d+1
.

For notational simplicity, we write

\mathcal{V}(s;t,x):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{s-t}}\int_{t}^{s}\left[\sigma^{-1}D\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(r)\right]^{\top}dB(r),

(52)

and

\mathcal{P}(T;t,x):=\int_{t}^{T}e^{-\beta(s-t)}\kappa\cdot d\left[D\tilde{Y}^{t,x}(s)\right].

(53)

Then, by the definition of
\bar{F}
, we have, for
j=1,2,\cdots,d
,

\displaystyle\left[\bar{F}\left(v^{(0)}\right)\right]_{j+1}(t,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\int_{t}^{T}\frac{e^{-\beta(s-t)}}{\sqrt{s-t}}\tilde{\cal H}\left(\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(s),v^{(0)}\right)\mathcal{V}_{j}(s;t,x)ds

(54)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{P}_{j}(T;t,x)+\frac{e^{-\beta(T-t)}}{\sqrt{T-t}}\xi\left(\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(T)\right)\mathcal{V}_{j}(T;t,x)\Bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\int_{t}^{T}\frac{e^{-\beta(s-t)}}{\sqrt{s-t}}\underline{c}\left(\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(s)\right)\mathcal{V}_{j}(s;t,x)ds

\displaystyle+\mathcal{P}_{j}(T;t,x)+\frac{e^{-\beta(T-t)}}{\sqrt{T-t}}\xi\left(\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(T)\right)\mathcal{V}_{j}(T;t,x)\Bigg],

where

\underline{c}(x)=\inf_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\left\{c(x,a)\right\},

(55)

and for
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\left[\bar{F}\left(v^{(\ell)}\right)-\bar{F}\left(v^{(\ell-1)}\right)\right]_{j+1}(t,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}\frac{e^{-\beta(s-t)}}{\sqrt{s-t}}\left[\tilde{\cal H}\left(\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(s),v^{(\ell)}\left(s,\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(s)\right)\right)-\tilde{\cal H}\left(\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(s),v^{(\ell-1)}\left(s,\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(s)\right)\right)\right]\mathcal{V}_{j}(s;t,x)ds\right].

(56)

We also have

\displaystyle\left[\bar{F}\left(v^{(0)}\right)\right]_{1}(t,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\int_{t}^{T}e^{-\beta(s-t)}\underline{c}\left(\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(s)\right)ds

(57)

\displaystyle\int_{t}^{T}e^{-\beta(s-t)}\kappa\cdot d\tilde{Y}^{t,x}(s)+e^{-\beta(T-t)}\xi\left(\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(T)\right)\Bigg],

and for
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\left[\bar{F}\left(v^{(\ell)}\right)-\bar{F}\left(v^{(\ell-1)}\right)\right]_{1}(t,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}e^{-\beta(s-t)}\left[\tilde{\cal H}\left(\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(s),v^{(\ell)}\left(s,\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(s)\right)\right)-\tilde{\cal H}\left(\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(s),v^{(\ell-1)}\left(s,\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(s)\right)\right)\right]ds\right].

(58)

We now make use of the identities in Eqs. (
54
), (
6.1
), (
57
) and (
58
)
to develop a multi-level Picard scheme
for approximating
(V^{(n)},v^{(n)})(t,x)
.

First, we develop unbiased estimators
for the expectations in (
54
) and (
6.1
). To this end, we treat
integration in time as expectation involving a randomly sampled time point.
More specifically, consider the integral
\int_{t}^{s}e^{-\beta(r-t)}(r-t)^{-1/2}dr
.
It can be computed that

\int_{t}^{s}e^{-\beta(r-t)}(r-t)^{-1/2}dr=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{\beta}}\left(1-2\Phi\left(-\sqrt{2\beta(s-t)}\right)\right),&\beta>0;\\
2\sqrt{s-t},&\beta=0.\end{array}\right.

(59)

where
\Phi(\cdot)
is the distribution function of the standard normal (with mean
0
and standard deviation
1
).
This motivates us to consider sampling a random time point
S\in(t,T)

with the following distribution:

\mathbb{P}(S<s)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{1-2\Phi\left(-\sqrt{2\beta(s-t)}\right)}{1-2\Phi\left(-\sqrt{2\beta(T-t)}\right)},&\beta>0;\\
\frac{\sqrt{s-t}}{\sqrt{T-t}},&\beta=0.\end{array}\right.

(60)

Also define the normalizing constant
C(\beta,T-t)
by

C(\beta,T-t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{\beta}}\left(1-2\Phi\left(-\sqrt{2\beta(T-t)}\right)\right),&\beta>0;\\
2\sqrt{T-t},&\beta=0.\end{array}\right.

(61)

When the context is clear, we often drop the
\beta
dependence and simply write
C(T-t)=C(\beta,T-t)
.
Then, we can rewrite Eq. (
54
) as

\displaystyle\left[\bar{F}\left(v^{(0)}\right)\right]_{j+1}(t,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}\Bigg[C(\beta,T-t)\cdot\underline{c}\left(\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(S)\right)\mathcal{V}_{j}(S;t,x)+\mathcal{P}_{j}(T;t,x)+\frac{e^{-\beta(T-t)}}{\sqrt{T-t}}\xi\left(\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(T)\right)\mathcal{V}_{j}(T;t,x)\Bigg],

(62)

Eq. (
6.1
) as

\displaystyle\left[\bar{F}\left(v^{(\ell)}\right)-\bar{F}\left(v^{(\ell-1)}\right)\right]_{j+1}(t,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}\left[C(\beta,T-t)\left(\tilde{\cal H}\left(\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(S),v^{(\ell)}\left(S,\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(S)\right)\right)-\tilde{\cal H}\left(\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(S),v^{(\ell-1)}\left(S,\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(S)\right)\right)\right)\mathcal{V}_{j}(S;t,x)\right],

(63)

Eq. (
57
) as

\displaystyle\left[\bar{F}\left(v^{(0)}\right)\right]_{1}(t,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}\Bigg[C(\beta,T-t)\cdot\sqrt{S-t}\cdot\underline{c}\left(\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(S)\right)+\int_{t}^{T}e^{-\beta(s-t)}\kappa\cdot d\tilde{Y}^{t,x}(s)+e^{-\beta(T-t)}\xi\left(\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(T)\right)\Bigg],

(64)

and Eq. (
58
) as

\displaystyle\left[\bar{F}\left(v^{(\ell)}\right)-\bar{F}\left(v^{(\ell-1)}\right)\right]_{1}(t,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}\left[C(\beta,T-t)\sqrt{S-t}\left(\tilde{\cal H}\left(\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(S),v^{(\ell)}\left(S,\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(S)\right)\right)-\tilde{\cal H}\left(\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(S),v^{(\ell-1)}\left(S,\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(S)\right)\right)\right)\right],

(65)

where
S
follows the distribution in Eq. (
60
),
independently from the process
\tilde{Z}^{t,x}
.

Second, in order to obtain estimates for
\bar{F}\left(v^{(0)}\right)(t,x)

and
\bar{F}\left(v^{(\ell)}\right)-\bar{F}\left(v^{(\ell-1)}\right)(t,x)
,
\ell=1,2,\cdots,n
,
we need to be able to efficiently and accurately simulate the following tuple:

\left(S,\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(S),\tilde{Z}^{t,x}(T),\int_{t}^{T}e^{-\beta(s-t)}\kappa\cdot d\tilde{Y}^{t,x}(s),\mathcal{P}(T;t,x),\mathcal{V}(S;t,x),\mathcal{V}(T;t,x)\right);

(66)

see expressions in Eqs. (
62
) – (
65
).
In addition, we also need to be able to efficiently compute the Hamiltonian
\tilde{\cal H}
(recall its definition in Eq. (
9
)),
since computing
\tilde{\cal H}(x,p)
for any given input
(x,p)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
requires us to solve an optimization problem
1
1

1

Note
that the computation of the gradient-dependent nonlinear term, in our case
\tilde{\cal H}(x,p)
, is often implicitly assumed to only take constant time
in prior literature. In stochastic control applications, this need not be the case.
.
These considerations motivate the following definitions.

Definition 6.1

(Polynomial-complexity simulation)
.

A simulation algorithm that generates one sample of the tuple (
66
), exactly or approximately,
has
polynomial computational complexity (in the dimension
d
)
, if there exist positive constants
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}

that are independent of
d
,
t
and
x
,
such that its expected runtime is no more than
\alpha_{1}\left(1+d^{\alpha_{2}}\right)
.

Definition 6.2
.

An optimization algorithm for solving
\tilde{\cal H}(x,p)
given any input
(x,p)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}

has
polynomial computational complexity (in the dimension
d
)
, if there exist positive constants
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}

that are independent of
d
,
x
, and
p
,
such that its expected runtime is no more than
\alpha_{1}\left(1+d^{\alpha_{2}}\right)
.

Note that Definition
6.1
does not require an algorithm to be accurate; in other words,
an algorithm for simulating the tuple (
66
) may have polynomial complexity and incur large simulation error at the same time.
In Section
D
, for the important special case
where components of
\tilde{Z}^{t,x}
are independent and
\kappa=0
,
we describe an exact simulation algorithm with polynomial complexity; in fact, the complexity is linear in
d
.
In more general settings where Euler–-Maruyama approximations are needed, there is an inherent tradeoff between efficiency,
as measured by runtime, and accuracy, as measured by simulation error.
If the number of discretization steps in an Euler–-Maruyama scheme is polynomial in
d
, then the scheme has polynomial complexity.
We leave the question of whether a polynomial number of discretization steps suffices to obtain
efficient MLP estimators with any prescribed accuracy as an important future research direction.

We are now ready to describe the MLP scheme.

Let
\Theta=\cup_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{Z}^{k}
be a parameter space,
and
n,M\in\mathbb{N}
be given hyperparameters.
Then define for all
t\in[0,T)
,
x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
, and
\theta\in\Theta
,
the following (random) functions recursively:

\left(V^{\theta}_{0,M},v^{\theta}_{0,M}\right)(t,x)\equiv 0;

(67)

and

\displaystyle\left(V^{\theta}_{n,M},v^{\theta}_{n,M}\right)(t,x)

(68)

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{M^{n}}\sum_{m=1}^{M^{n}}\Bigg[C(\beta,T-t)\cdot\underline{c}\left(\tilde{Z}^{t,x}_{(\theta,0,m)}\left(S^{(\theta,0,m)}\right)\right)\left(\sqrt{S^{(\theta,0,m)}-t},\mathcal{V}^{(\theta,0,m)}\left(S^{(\theta,0,m)};t,x\right)\right)

(69)

\displaystyle+\left(\int_{t}^{T}e^{-\beta(s-t)}\kappa\cdot d\tilde{Y}^{t,x}_{(\theta,0,m)}(s),\mathcal{P}^{(\theta,0,m)}(T;t,x)\right)

(70)

\displaystyle+\frac{e^{-\beta(T-t)}}{\sqrt{T-t}}\xi\left(\tilde{Z}^{t,x}_{(\theta,0,m)}(T)\right)\left(\sqrt{T-t},\mathcal{V}^{(\theta,0,m)}\left(T;t,x\right)\right)\Bigg]

(71)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\ell=1}^{n-1}\sum_{m=1}^{M^{n-\ell}}\frac{C(\beta,T-t)}{M^{n-\ell}}\left(\sqrt{S^{(\theta,\ell,m)}-t},\mathcal{V}{(\theta,\ell,m)}\left(S^{(\theta,\ell,m)};t,x\right)\right)\times

(72)
